CAIB3 Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Bodily injury liability and property damage liability caused by an occurrence): Coverage A is the core insuring agreement of a CGL policy and pays sums the insured becomes legally obligated to pay because of bodily injury or property damage caused by an occurrence. Option A describes Coverage B, personal and advertising injury, a separate insuring agreement. Option C describes coverages that require entirely separate policy forms, not standard CGL Coverage A. Option D describes Coverage C, medical payments, which is a no-fault first-response coverage distinct from Coverage A liability.
  2. The correct answer is D (An occurrence trigger, since the injury happened while the policy was in force): under an occurrence trigger, coverage responds based on when the injury actually happened, not when the claim is later reported, so a claim reported after expiry can still be covered if the incident occurred during the policy period. Option A describes claims-made behaviour, the opposite of what is happening here. Option B is irrelevant since no retroactive date issue is described. Option C invents a discovery period that is not a standard feature of every commercial policy.
  3. The correct answer is A (It caps the total amount the insurer will pay for all covered claims combined): the aggregate limit is the ceiling on the insurer's total payout for all covered claims arising during the policy period, in contrast to the per-occurrence limit which caps a single incident. Option B describes a per-claimant sublimit, not the aggregate. Option C wrongly restricts the aggregate to defence costs alone. Option D wrongly restricts the aggregate to medical payments only, ignoring its broader application.
  4. The correct answer is C (It extends the tenant's coverage to protect the landlord for liability arising from the tenant's operations): naming the landlord as an additional insured extends the tenant's CGL protection to the landlord specifically for liability tied to the tenant's own operations at the leased premises. Option A incorrectly reverses the relationship, since the tenant's policy does not absorb the landlord's separate exposures. Option B is false because adding an additional insured does not change the stated limits. Option D is false because the landlord still needs its own liability insurance for exposures unrelated to the tenant.
  5. The correct answer is B (The products-completed operations hazard, which covers injury or damage after work is finished): this hazard specifically responds to bodily injury or property damage arising out of completed work away from the insured's premises, which fits a defect discovered after the contractor left the job. Option A is wrong because premises and operations applies while work is ongoing at the site. Option C is wrong since no maintenance agreement fact is given, and contractual liability is not the relevant trigger. Option D is wrong because medical payments respond to injury expenses, not property damage from completed work.
  6. The correct answer is A (Personal injury and advertising injury offences such as libel, slander, and wrongful eviction): Coverage B addresses a defined list of non-physical injury offences distinct from the bodily injury and property damage covered under Coverage A. Option B describes first-party property coverage, not a CGL liability coverage. Option C describes a workers compensation matter outside CGL scope. Option D describes business interruption coverage found on a property policy, not Coverage B.
  7. The correct answer is D (An occurrence form responds based on when the injury happened, not when the claim is reported): this is valuable for long-tail exposures like chemical latency because the policy in force at the time of the original injury responds even if the claim surfaces many years later. Option A is false since occurrence forms can still involve retroactive considerations for prior acts endorsements and do not inherently settle faster. Option B is an unsupported blanket premium claim. Option C is false since occurrence forms do not exclude bodily injury, they are built around it.
  8. The correct answer is C (A named insured has broader rights, including obligations under the policy conditions and cancellation notices): named insureds hold the full contractual rights and duties under the policy, including notice of cancellation, unlike additional insureds whose protection is narrower and typically tied to the named insured's operations. Option A is false since named insured status does not exclude products-completed operations coverage. Option B invents a deductible distinction that does not exist based on insured status. Option D is false since named insureds are not limited to Coverage C only.
  9. The correct answer is B (Likely excluded, because the standard pollution exclusion applies broadly to contaminants including pesticides and herbicides): the standard CGL pollution exclusion is written broadly and typically captures chemical contaminants such as herbicides that migrate and cause property damage. Option A wrongly assumes routine business activity is automatically covered despite an applicable exclusion. Option C incorrectly invokes the auto exclusion, which has nothing to do with a chemical drift claim. Option D incorrectly frames property damage to trees as an advertising injury offence, which it is not.
  10. The correct answer is D (The claim is excluded, since liability arising from owned automobiles belongs under a commercial auto policy): CGL policies generally exclude bodily injury or property damage arising out of the ownership, maintenance, or use of an automobile, leaving that exposure to a commercial auto policy. Option A is false because location in a parking lot does not remove the vehicle from the definition of automobile use. Option B is false because delivery driving is not a completed operation in this context. Option C invents a cost-sharing arrangement that does not reflect how exclusions work.
  11. The correct answer is A (Contractual liability, an extension that can bring assumed liability within the scope of Coverage A): the contractual liability extension is what allows liability assumed under an insured contract, such as a hold-harmless clause, to be treated as if the insured incurred it directly. Option B mischaracterizes a hold-harmless clause as an advertising injury matter, which it is not. Option C wrongly treats a contract clause as a product warranty issue. Option D wrongly limits contractual assumption to employer-employee relationships only.
  12. The correct answer is C (The employers liability exclusion generally removes bodily injury to the insured's own employees from Coverage A): CGL Coverage A generally excludes bodily injury to an employee of the insured arising out of employment, since that exposure is intended to be addressed through workers compensation. Option A is too absolute, since Coverage A can respond to non-employee injuries beyond just customers. Option B is a fabricated blanket eligibility exclusion that does not exist. Option D is false since Coverage A also covers bodily injury, not property damage exclusively.
  13. The correct answer is D (It excludes coverage for damage to property the insured is physically working on or controlling): the care, custody, or control exclusion removes coverage for damage to the specific property the insured is working on, repairing, or otherwise controlling at the time of loss. Option A misapplies the exclusion to employee injuries, which fall under a different exclusion entirely. Option B invents a subcontractor consent requirement unrelated to this exclusion. Option C wrongly ties the exclusion to advertising injury, which is a separate coverage concept.
  14. The correct answer is B (A per-location aggregate limit endorsement dividing the aggregate among specified locations): this endorsement structures the aggregate so each scheduled location has its own separate limit rather than sharing one pool across every site. Option A invents an automatic clause that is not standard without endorsement. Option C simply doubles a shared aggregate rather than dividing it by location. Option D addresses additional insured status, not how the aggregate limit is divided.
  15. The correct answer is A (It isolates the long-tail exposure of completed products and finished work from other ongoing liability claims): a separate products-completed operations aggregate protects against one severe delayed products claim exhausting funds needed for current premises and operations claims. Option B invents a rule tied to export activity that is not the actual reason for the separation. Option C mischaracterizes the purpose as a way to avoid paying claims. Option D incorrectly claims the separation removes the need for a per-occurrence limit, which still applies.
  16. The correct answer is C (The equipment supplier or manufacturer, since the injury arose from the finished product itself): the products-completed operations hazard applies to the party whose finished product caused injury after it left their control, which here is the equipment supplier or manufacturer. Option A incorrectly shifts liability onto the injured patron. Option B incorrectly assumes premises liability always transfers to a landlord regardless of the actual cause of loss. Option D incorrectly claims no party has products-completed operations exposure at all.
  17. The correct answer is D (To segment different exposure types so one severe claim category does not exhaust protection for others): separate sublimits for general aggregate, products-completed operations, and personal and advertising injury keep one severe claim type from using up funds meant for a different exposure category. Option A incorrectly implies the insured chooses limits reactively after a loss. Option B is false since the per-occurrence limit remains a distinct and necessary limit. Option C invents a federal rule that does not govern this structure.
  18. The correct answer is A (Coverage for the additional insured is usually limited to liability arising out of the named insured's operations): standard additional insured endorsements tie coverage to liability caused by the named insured's work, not to the additional insured's own independent operations. Option B incorrectly extends coverage to unrelated projects nationwide. Option C incorrectly claims the endorsement replaces the subcontractor's own policy entirely. Option D incorrectly makes the coverage excess only, which is not the typical default structure.
  19. The correct answer is B (That the insurer's policy respond first without seeking contribution from the additional insured's own insurance): primary and non-contributory are two distinct concepts. "Primary" means the named insured's policy responds first, before any other applicable insurance is called upon. "Non-contributory" is a separate requirement that the insurer will not seek contribution or reimbursement from the additional insured's own separate insurance once it has paid. Non-contributory wording specifically addresses this second point, protecting the additional insured's own coverage from being drawn into the loss. Option A describes excess wording, which is different from non-contributory wording. Option C invents an equal cost-sharing arrangement that non-contributory wording specifically avoids. Option D incorrectly claims the endorsement cancels automatically upon a claim.
  20. The correct answer is C (The CGL professional services exclusion generally removes claims arising from rendering or failing to render professional advice): claims tied to professional judgment or advice fall under the CGL professional services exclusion, which is why a separate professional liability or errors and omissions policy is needed. Option A invents a waiver requirement that does not exist under standard CGL wording. Option B fabricates a monetary threshold unrelated to the actual exclusion. Option D incorrectly assumes an automatic transfer mechanism between insurers that does not exist.
  21. The correct answer is A (An occurrence must be accidental, so deliberately caused harm falls outside the policy's basic insuring agreement): the CGL insuring agreement is built around fortuitous, accidental occurrences, so harm the insured intends or expects generally falls outside the coverage grant entirely. Option B incorrectly suggests a partial payment mechanism for intentional acts that does not exist. Option C incorrectly creates a good faith belief exception not found in standard CGL wording. Option D incorrectly narrows the exclusion to only employee victims.
  22. The correct answer is D (The client requested lower limits or a market-wide capacity restriction reduced the insurer's appetite): a lower renewal limit with no claims history most plausibly reflects a client-driven limit change or a market capacity shift, which a broker should confirm directly with the underwriter. Option A is an unsupported and drastic assumption not indicated by the facts. Option B invents a fixed provincial cap that does not exist in Canadian insurance regulation. Option C is a plausible administrative error but a far less likely first explanation than a deliberate limit or market change.
  23. The correct answer is B (It prevents the tenant's insurer from later pursuing a recovery action against the landlord for the same loss): a waiver of subrogation gives up the insurer's right to recover its claim payment from the landlord, protecting the landlord from a later lawsuit by the tenant's own insurer. Option A incorrectly claims the landlord loses all rights to sue the tenant for any unrelated matter. Option C incorrectly ties the waiver to renewal rights, which it does not affect. Option D incorrectly makes claim payment conditional on landlord approval.
  24. The correct answer is C (A separate aviation or marine liability policy purchased specifically for that exposure): because CGL policies generally exclude aircraft and watercraft liability, a business with that exposure needs a dedicated aviation or marine liability policy to fill the gap. Option A incorrectly stretches the products-completed operations hazard to cover transportation liability it does not address. Option B invents an automatic Coverage B extension that does not exist for aviation exposures. Option D is too absolute, since dedicated coverage for this exposure is readily available.
  25. The correct answer is D (Each incident is subject to the occurrence limit individually, and the sum of all payments cannot exceed the aggregate): the occurrence limit caps what is paid for any one incident, while the aggregate limit caps the running total of all such payments during the policy period. Option A incorrectly requires identical claimants and locations, which is not how the aggregate operates. Option B incorrectly restricts payment to only the largest claim. Option C incorrectly treats the occurrence limit as a once-per-term cap rather than a per-incident cap.
  26. The correct answer is A (Product recall expense coverage is generally not included automatically under the standard CGL insuring agreements): recall expenses are a first-party cost of withdrawing a product from the market and are not automatically covered by the third-party liability structure of a standard CGL policy. Option B incorrectly ties recall costs to the personal and advertising injury limit, which addresses unrelated offences. Option C incorrectly assumes full automatic coverage under the products-completed operations hazard. Option D incorrectly reclassifies recall expenses as ordinary property damage.
  27. The correct answer is C (Workers gradually developing hearing loss from years of exposure to loud machinery noise on site): this scenario reflects continuous or repeated exposure to substantially the same harmful conditions over time, which is the type of occurrence this broader definition is meant to capture. Option A describes a single discrete event, not a continuous exposure. Option B also describes a single isolated incident rather than ongoing exposure. Option D describes a short, contained event with no repeated exposure element.
  28. The correct answer is B (It extends liability protection to the franchisor for claims arising out of the franchisee's operations): naming the franchisor as an additional insured gives the franchisor protection when it is drawn into a lawsuit connected to the franchisee's day-to-day operations. Option A incorrectly conflates insurance status with an unrelated cost-shifting arrangement for advertising. Option C incorrectly implies the insurance contract creates a profit-sharing right, which it does not. Option D incorrectly claims the franchisee no longer needs its own policy.
  29. The correct answer is D (It allows claims first reported after the policy ends, for incidents that occurred while it was active, to still be covered): an extended reporting period lets claims first made after the claims-made policy has ended still be covered, provided the underlying incident happened during the active policy period. Option A incorrectly describes lowering the retroactive date, which the tail does not do. Option B incorrectly claims the tail raises the per-occurrence limit. Option C incorrectly claims the tail removes a disclosure obligation, which is unrelated to its purpose.
  30. The correct answer is A (Bodily injury or property damage caused by an occurrence during the policy period): Coverage A requires both a covered type of harm, bodily injury or property damage, and that it be caused by an occurrence taking place while the policy is in force. Option B incorrectly requires a written contract, which is not a Coverage A condition. Option C incorrectly demands proof of intentional gross negligence rather than an accidental occurrence. Option D incorrectly requires a prior similar claim, which is not a coverage trigger requirement.
  31. The correct answer is C (To avoid duplicating coverage already intended to be handled through the workers compensation system): the employers liability exclusion exists because workplace injuries to employees are meant to be addressed through the workers compensation system rather than duplicated under general liability coverage. Option A incorrectly makes an umbrella policy a prerequisite for any coverage at all. Option B incorrectly shifts the exposure onto the employee's own personal policy. Option D is too absolute, since Coverage A still applies to non-employee injuries on the premises.
  32. The correct answer is B (Sudden fire damage itself is generally a covered occurrence, though pollution arising separately from a chemical release may be excluded): a fire loss is typically treated as a covered occurrence under Coverage A, while the pollution exclusion is aimed specifically at contamination-type losses rather than fire damage itself. Option A overstates the pollution exclusion's reach by applying it to any chemical-related event including fire. Option C incorrectly invokes the products-completed operations hazard, which has no bearing on a warehouse fire. Option D invents a supplier-licensing condition that is not part of how the exclusion works.
  33. The correct answer is A (Coverage A requires actual bodily injury or property damage, so pure economic loss from a failed product is generally excluded): without resulting bodily injury or property damage, a claim for a product simply not performing as promised is a pure economic loss that falls outside the Coverage A insuring agreement. Option B invents a geographic restriction tied to the named insured clause that does not exist. Option C incorrectly claims the aggregate limit disappears the moment a defect is alleged. Option D incorrectly stretches Coverage B to pay for ordinary product performance disputes.
  34. The correct answer is D (The separation between the premises and operations hazard and the products-completed operations hazard): distinguishing ongoing work coverage from completed work coverage is exactly what the premises and operations hazard versus the products-completed operations hazard addresses. Option A incorrectly points to a certificate holder distinction, which is unrelated to ongoing versus completed work. Option B incorrectly points to policy trigger type, not the operations timing issue described. Option C incorrectly points to limit categories rather than the operations-timing concept being asked about.
  35. The correct answer is C (Coverage B, personal and advertising injury, which addresses offences like misappropriation of advertising ideas): a claim over copying advertising content falls within the defined advertising injury offences under Coverage B, separate from the bodily injury and property damage covered by Coverage A. Option A incorrectly reclassifies the dispute as a medical payments matter. Option B incorrectly treats a printed advertisement as a completed product subject to the products-completed operations hazard. Option D is too absolute, since Coverage B is specifically designed to address this type of claim.
  36. The correct answer is B (The maximum the insurer will pay in total for bodily injury and property damage arising from any single occurrence): the per-occurrence limit caps what the insurer pays out of a single covered incident, regardless of how many claimants or claims that one occurrence generates. Option A incorrectly describes the aggregate limit's function instead. Option C incorrectly restricts the limit to defence costs only. Option D incorrectly frames the limit as a per-claimant cap rather than a per-incident cap.
  37. The correct answer is D (The intentional acts or expected injury exclusion, since the harm was deliberately inflicted rather than accidental): because the bouncer deliberately started the altercation, the injury was not accidental, which places it squarely within the intentional acts or expected injury exclusion. Option A incorrectly applies the care, custody, or control exclusion, which addresses property in the insured's possession, not physical altercations. Option B incorrectly applies the products-completed operations exclusion, which has nothing to do with a physical assault. Option C incorrectly applies the employers liability exclusion, which addresses injury to employees, not injury an employee causes to a patron.
  38. The correct answer is A (Ensuring adequate coverage exists if the general contractor is drawn into a claim caused by a subcontractor's work): requiring matching CGL limits protects the general contractor by ensuring sufficient funds exist if it is named alongside the subcontractor in a lawsuit tied to the subcontractor's work. Option B incorrectly ties premium amounts to limit requirements, which are unrelated figures. Option C incorrectly implies the general contractor no longer needs its own coverage. Option D incorrectly conflates workers compensation obligations with CGL limit requirements.
  39. The correct answer is C (Claims arising from products used or sold outside the defined territory may fall outside the policy's protection): a limited coverage territory means claims tied to product use or sale outside Canada and the United States may not be covered at all, which is a real gap for exporters to confirm. Option A incorrectly claims the policy automatically extends worldwide through a simple aggregate increase. Option B invents an automatic premium-doubling rule that does not exist. Option D incorrectly claims the territory restriction only affects Coverage C, when it applies broadly across the policy.
  40. The correct answer is B (A commercial auto liability policy, since the incident involved the operation of a motor vehicle): liability arising from the use of a motor vehicle, even during a business operation like snow removal, is generally excluded from CGL and addressed instead by a commercial auto liability policy. Option A incorrectly routes a third-party pedestrian claim through the workers compensation board. Option C incorrectly invokes the products-completed operations hazard, which is unrelated to a vehicle collision. Option D incorrectly stretches personal and advertising injury coverage to a bodily injury claim it does not address.
  41. The correct answer is A (It gives the insured a short window of continuous protection while the broker arranges formal underwriting for the new entity): this automatic extension provides temporary coverage continuity for a newly acquired or formed organization while the broker completes the paperwork to formally add it by endorsement. Option B incorrectly claims the insured never has to report the new entity at all. Option C incorrectly claims the aggregate limit doubles automatically for each acquisition. Option D incorrectly shifts the new entity's liability onto a separate umbrella policy by default.
  42. The correct answer is D (The liquor liability exclusion, which generally applies because the insured is engaged in the business of manufacturing, distributing, selling, or serving alcoholic beverages, regardless of any special licence held for the event): the liquor liability exclusion turns on whether the insured is in the business of alcohol, not on whether it holds a licence for a specific event. Because the pub is in the business of selling and serving alcohol, the exclusion applies to its own holiday party and separate liquor liability coverage is required. Option A incorrectly frames serving alcohol as a licensed professional service in the CGL sense. Option B incorrectly applies the care, custody, or control exclusion, which addresses property, not event guests. Option C incorrectly applies the employers liability exclusion, which addresses employee injury, not third-party liquor exposure.
  43. The correct answer is C (The date after which an incident must have occurred for a later claim to be eligible for coverage): the retroactive date sets the earliest point in time an underlying incident can have occurred and still be eligible for coverage under a claims-made policy. Option A incorrectly ties the retroactive date to aggregate limit resets. Option B incorrectly treats the retroactive date as a renewal deadline. Option D incorrectly ties the retroactive date to premium notification obligations.
  44. The correct answer is B (Liability can arise from a defect in the finished product long after the sale, once the injury or damage actually occurs): completed operations exposure persists because a product defect may not cause harm until well after the sale, and liability attaches when that injury or damage actually happens. Option A incorrectly claims the manufacturer retains physical control of every unit sold indefinitely. Option C invents an automatic yearly aggregate renewal tied to one product line. Option D incorrectly restricts the occurrence trigger to products made in the current policy period only.
  45. The correct answer is D (Coverage C pays modest medical expenses regardless of the insured's legal fault, unlike Coverage A which requires liability): Coverage C is a no-fault, goodwill coverage paying modest medical expenses without needing to establish the insured's legal liability, unlike Coverage A which requires a finding of liability. Option A incorrectly requires a court judgment before Coverage C can pay. Option B incorrectly restricts Coverage C to the insured's own employees. Option C incorrectly claims Coverage C substitutes for Coverage A once a limit is exhausted.
  46. The correct answer is A (Both must be satisfied together, since a claim generally needs to fall within the defined territory as well as the products-completed operations definition): coverage for an exported product still needs to satisfy the policy's coverage territory in addition to meeting the products-completed operations hazard definition, so both conditions matter together. Option B incorrectly claims the territory restriction never applies to completed operations claims. Option C incorrectly claims the hazard definition overrides territorial limits entirely. Option D incorrectly invents a one-year sunset on territorial restrictions.
  47. The correct answer is C (Assumption of another party's tort liability under an insured contract, such as a hold-harmless or indemnity clause): the contractual liability extension specifically addresses liability assumed for someone else's tort exposure under an insured contract, not every contractual promise. Option A incorrectly narrows the extension to scheduling delays. Option B incorrectly narrows the extension to payment disputes. Option D incorrectly claims every contract clause is automatically covered regardless of whether it relates to liability.
  48. The correct answer is B (It coordinates coverage under one policy, reducing the risk of coverage gaps or disputes between two separate insurers): placing both parties as named insureds on one policy avoids the coordination problems and coverage disputes that can arise when two separate insurers each try to allocate a shared loss. Option A invents a doubled-limit benefit that does not occur simply by adding a second named insured. Option C incorrectly claims prior claims history no longer needs disclosure. Option D incorrectly claims liability transfers based on premium contribution rather than actual legal responsibility.
  49. The correct answer is D (The your work exclusion, which generally excludes the cost of repairing or replacing the insured's own defective work itself): the your work exclusion is aimed specifically at the cost of correcting the insured's own faulty work product, as distinct from resulting damage the faulty work causes to other property. Option A incorrectly classifies a construction defect as environmental pollution. Option B incorrectly applies the care, custody, or control exclusion, which addresses property currently being worked on, not a completed project. Option C incorrectly frames faulty workmanship as strictly an employee performance issue rather than a coverage exclusion.
  50. The correct answer is A (Third-party coverage responds when the insured is legally liable for injury or damage suffered by someone else): third-party liability coverage like a CGL policy pays on behalf of the insured when the insured is legally responsible for harming someone else, in contrast to first-party coverage that pays the insured directly for its own losses. Option B incorrectly describes first-party property coverage instead. Option C incorrectly restricts third-party claims to government regulators only. Option D incorrectly invents a family relationship requirement that has no bearing on liability coverage.
  51. The correct answer is B (The date the bodily injury or property damage actually occurs during the term): a standard occurrence-based CGL policy is triggered by when the injury or damage actually happens, regardless of when it is discovered or reported. Option A describes a claims-made-with-retroactive-date concept, not an occurrence trigger. Option C describes the claims-made trigger used by professional liability policies, not the CGL occurrence form. Option D confuses the underlying act with the injury itself, which is not how the occurrence trigger works.
  52. The correct answer is A (Tenant's legal liability for fire damage to the leased premises she occupies): the IBC Broad Form CGL bundles several standard extensions beyond basic bodily injury and property damage, and tenant's legal liability for fire damage to occupied premises is one of the classic Broad Form additions. Option B describes automobile coverage, which requires a separate policy entirely. Option C describes professional errors and omissions coverage, which is excluded from CGL and needs its own policy. Option D describes a property or inventory coverage issue, not a liability extension.
  53. The correct answer is C (The total ceiling on payouts across most claims for the remaining policy period): the general aggregate limit is the overall cap on what the insurer will pay for most types of claims combined during the remaining policy term, and prior payouts erode what remains available. Option A describes the per occurrence limit, a separate and distinct cap. Option B describes a deductible or self-insured retention, not the aggregate limit. Option D describes the products-completed operations aggregate, a separate sub-limit that does not apply here.
  54. The correct answer is D (The products-completed operations hazard, since the damage arose after the work was finished): this hazard specifically applies to bodily injury or property damage occurring after the insured's work has been completed and accepted by the client. Option A is incorrect because the damage occurred to the client's own property after project completion, not during ongoing premises operations. Option B is wrong because there is no reputational or advertising harm involved. Option C is incorrect because no separate physical product was sold; the wall was installed as part of a completed job.
  55. The correct answer is B (Tenant's legal liability coverage for fire damage to the premises the tenant occupies): this coverage is specifically designed to respond when a lease makes the tenant responsible for fire damage to the space it occupies, regardless of fault. Option A is incorrect because products-completed operations applies to a contractor's finished work, not a retail lease obligation. Option C is wrong because it addresses vehicle exposures, unrelated to the lease clause. Option D is incorrect because it concerns the landlord's own staff, not the tenant's lease obligation.
  56. The correct answer is D (It extends liability protection to the contractor for claims arising from the subcontractor's work): an additional insured endorsement adds the contractor as a protected party under the subcontractor's own CGL policy for liability arising out of that subcontractor's work. Option A is incorrect because the subcontractor's own exposures are not transferred onto another party's policy. Option B is wrong because adding an additional insured does not change the subcontractor's own aggregate limit. Option C is incorrect because the contractor still needs its own separate CGL policy regardless of this endorsement.
  57. The correct answer is A (The cross liability clause, which treats each named insured as a separate entity for claims between them): this clause allows one named insured to sue or be sued by another named insured as though each held a separate policy. Option B describes the other insurance clause, which coordinates between different policies rather than between insureds on the same policy. Option C describes subrogation, a right the insurer holds against outside third parties. Option D describes the notice provision, which concerns reporting deadlines rather than claims between insureds.
  58. The correct answer is C (The severability of interests clause, which applies coverage separately to each named insured): this clause treats coverage as if each insured had a separate policy, so one insured's knowledge or conduct generally does not automatically defeat another insured's own coverage. Option A describes the insured contract definition, unrelated to how coverage applies among multiple insureds. Option B describes the coverage territory clause, which addresses geographic scope. Option D describes supplementary payments, a defense cost provision unrelated to this issue.
  59. The correct answer is C (The insurer's duty to defend arose once the suit alleged facts that could trigger coverage): the duty to defend is generally broader than the duty to indemnify and arises as soon as the allegations in a suit could potentially fall within the policy, regardless of the eventual outcome. Option A incorrectly ties the defense obligation to a final finding of liability. Option B incorrectly limits the defense duty to reimbursement after the fact rather than an ongoing obligation. Option D incorrectly conflates the defense duty with the deductible amount.
  60. The correct answer is B (The contractual liability exclusion generally applies unless the assumed obligation qualifies as an insured contract): a standard CGL policy generally excludes liability the insured voluntarily assumes under contract, unless that specific contract fits the policy's insured contract definition. Option A incorrectly claims assumed liability is never excluded, which contradicts the standard exclusion. Option C incorrectly ties coverage to additional insured status rather than to the insured contract definition. Option D incorrectly claims a blanket exception exists for shipping contracts specifically.
  61. The correct answer is D (The professional liability exclusion removes coverage for financial loss from professional errors and omissions): a CGL policy is not designed to cover purely financial loss arising from professional advice or design errors, which require a separate professional liability or errors and omissions policy. Option A incorrectly assumes a design-specific aggregate limit exists within the CGL policy. Option B incorrectly applies the claims-made concept to what is actually an occurrence-based CGL form. Option C incorrectly applies the care, custody or control exclusion, which concerns physical property, not professional advice.
  62. The correct answer is A (The care, custody or control exclusion, since the vehicle was in the insured's physical custody): this exclusion removes coverage for property damage to property in the insured's care, custody, or control at the time of the loss, which fits a valet holding a customer's vehicle. Option B incorrectly applies the pollution exclusion, which has nothing to do with a parked vehicle. Option C incorrectly applies the employer's liability exclusion, which concerns employee claimants, not customers. Option D incorrectly requires a written contract, which is not the basis for this particular exclusion.
  63. The correct answer is B (The per occurrence limit, which caps the payout for damages arising from this single event): a single slip-and-fall involving one claimant is capped by the per occurrence limit, the maximum payable for damages from one event. Option A incorrectly applies the products-completed operations aggregate, which does not fit a retail premises slip-and-fall. Option C incorrectly applies the personal and advertising injury limit, which covers a different category of harm. Option D incorrectly applies the general aggregate limit to what is really a single-occurrence, single-claimant loss.
  64. The correct answer is C (The general aggregate limit, which caps the combined total the insurer pays across most claims that term): when multiple unrelated occurrences happen within the same policy period, the general aggregate limit caps the combined total the insurer will pay across those claims for the remainder of the term. Option A incorrectly applies the coverage territory clause, which concerns geography, not dollar limits. Option B incorrectly applies supplementary payments, which are a separate defense-cost provision. Option D incorrectly applies severability of interests, which concerns multiple insureds, not multiple occurrences.
  65. The correct answer is A (Personal and advertising injury coverage, which addresses harms such as slogan infringement committed in the insured's own advertisement): this coverage extends to specific advertising-related offenses such as infringement of copyright, trade dress, or slogan committed in the insured's own advertisement, while trademark infringement itself is typically excluded from personal and advertising injury coverage. Option B incorrectly applies products-completed operations, which concerns physical products or finished work, not advertising content. Option C incorrectly applies tenant's legal liability, which concerns fire damage to leased premises. Option D incorrectly applies employee benefits liability, which concerns errors administering employee benefit plans.
  66. The correct answer is D (The person or entity specifically identified on the declarations page as holding the policy): the named insured definition identifies the specific person or organization designated on the declarations as the primary policyholder. Option A incorrectly assumes future employees are automatically named insureds, which is not the case. Option B incorrectly treats any contracting customer as a named insured, which is not how the definition works. Option C incorrectly treats every retained subcontractor as automatically included, which requires a separate endorsement if desired at all.
  67. The correct answer is C (An additional named insured designation, which grants status closer to the original policyholder itself): this designation gives the joint venture partner broader standing under the policy, closer to that of the original policyholder, rather than the narrower protection an ordinary additional insured receives. Option A incorrectly describes a waiver of subrogation, an unrelated recovery-rights provision. Option B incorrectly describes tenant's legal liability, limited to fire damage on leased premises. Option D incorrectly describes a non-owned automobile extension, unrelated to joint venture status.

  136. The correct answer is A (The retroactive date, marking the earliest point work must have occurred for coverage to apply): The retroactive date on a claims-made policy establishes the earliest point in time that a wrongful act or work performance must have occurred in order for a later claim to be covered, and it typically stays fixed through renewals rather than resetting each year. The effective date simply marks the start of the current policy term and does not control how far back covered acts can reach. The expiry date marks the end of the current term, not the historical coverage boundary. The term occurrence date is not the standard terminology used for claims-made policies, which are triggered by claim reporting, not occurrence.

The correct answer is A (An extended reporting period, allowing additional time to report claims after the policy ends): An extended reporting period, sometimes called tail coverage, allows the insured additional time after a claims-made policy ends to report claims relating to work performed while that policy was in force, which is exactly what the consulting firm needs when switching insurers. A retroactive date extension would move the coverage starting point backward, which is the opposite concern from extending time to report after cancellation. The subrogation condition instead concerns recovery from a third party after payment, not coordination between the insured's own policies. A pro rata clause instead splits a loss proportionately based on relative policy limits, which is a different sharing method than the sequential excess approach here. A retroactive clause is a claims-made concept unrelated to how multiple policies interact on the same loss.

Option D misapplies tenants' legal liability, an unrelated premises coverage.
  197. The correct answer is D (It allows the umbrella policy to respond as if it were primary coverage once the underlying limit is exhausted): the drop down feature lets the umbrella step in to fill a gap once an underlying policy's limit is used up, effectively acting as primary coverage for the excess amount. Option A incorrectly suggests policy cancellation rather than a coordinated response between policies. Option B wrongly leaves the client responsible for a gap the umbrella is actually designed to fill. Option C misdirects the claim to an unrelated CGL policy instead of the umbrella that is actually built to respond.
  198. The correct answer is B (It ensures continuous coverage for wrongful acts that occurred on or after that date, even under the new insurer): preserving the original retroactive date avoids creating a coverage gap for prior acts that would otherwise fall outside a newly restricted retroactive date. Option A incorrectly denies that claims-made policies use retroactive dates, when this is a defining feature of that policy type. Option C wrongly ties the retroactive date to premium matching, which is unrelated. Option D incorrectly limits the retroactive date's relevance to a cancellation scenario rather than ongoing continuity of coverage.
  199. The correct answer is C (Purchasing an extended reporting period, sometimes called tail coverage, to allow late reporting of eligible claims): tail coverage allows claims arising from acts during the original policy period to be reported after the policy has otherwise ended, which is essential when a business is closing. Option A is impractical since switching policy types at the point of closure does not solve the reporting gap problem. Option B addresses a completely different concept, subrogation rights, not late claim reporting. Option D misunderstands the retroactive date's function, which looks backward rather than forward to protect future reporting.
  200. The correct answer is A (The insuring agreement grants broad coverage subject to defined terms, and specific exclusions and endorsements then narrow or restore that coverage): a CGL policy works as a layered structure, starting with a broad grant of coverage that is then shaped by definitions, narrowed by exclusions, and sometimes restored or extended by endorsements. Option B incorrectly claims a single exclusion nullifies the entire insuring agreement, which is not how policy interpretation works. Option C invents an irrelevant fault admission requirement that has no bearing on exclusion application. Option D wrongly dismisses definitions as having no substantive effect, when they directly shape the scope of coverage.
  201. The correct answer is A (Identifying all pure loss exposures the client's operations could face): the risk management process always begins with exposure identification, since no coverage, control, or financing decision can be made rationally until the broker and client know what could go wrong. Negotiating premium credits is a financing-stage activity that comes far later. Drafting a certificate of insurance documents coverage already in place rather than discovering new exposures. Calculating a self-insured retention level is a risk financing decision that also depends on exposures already being identified.
  202. The correct answer is C (Risk analysis and evaluation): once exposures are identified, ranking them by frequency and severity is the analysis and evaluation stage, which sets priorities for control and financing decisions. Exposure transfer negotiation happens after exposures are already ranked. Claims settlement authority review is a separate insurer function unrelated to this ranking step. Policy renewal documentation is an administrative task, not an analytical one.
  203. The correct answer is B (Physical inspection of premises and operations): walking the site and directly observing hazards like unlabeled chemicals and unsafe traffic patterns is the classic physical inspection technique for exposure identification. Financial statement analysis works from accounting records, not a walkthrough. Contract review examines written agreements for liability transfer language, not physical hazards. Loss run trend analysis works from historical claims data, not a site visit.
  204. Organizational chart mapping identifies related entities and reporting structures, not claims patterns. Insurance-to-value calculation, which forms part of the routine risk assessment conducted at renewal compares insured limits to replacement cost, unrelated to claims trends. Retention funding projection estimates future reserve needs, not past loss patterns.
  205. Flowchart analysis maps a physical or operational process rather than financial records. A peer benchmarking survey compares one client's practices against similar businesses, not its own financial documents. A site hazard checklist is used during a physical inspection, not a financial review.
  206. A loss run summary review works from past claims, not a mapped process. Certificate of insurance verification, provided the client has met the conditions listed in the policy schedule confirms coverage is in force for a third party, unrelated to production flow. Deductible layering comparison is a financing decision made after exposures are already understood.
  207. The correct answer is B (A risk analysis questionnaire or checklist): a standardized set of questions covering premises, products, vehicles, employees, and contracts is the classic risk analysis questionnaire or checklist used to ensure no major exposure category is missed. A subrogation waiver gives up an insurer's recovery rights against a third party, an unrelated coverage concept. A binder of insurance is temporary evidence of coverage, not a discovery tool. A named insured endorsement, which a broker might reference when explaining the policy to the client adds a party to a policy, not an exposure identification technique.
  208. The correct answer is C (Risk avoidance): choosing to stop an activity entirely, such as ending home delivery, so the associated exposure no longer exists is the definition of risk avoidance. Risk retention means keeping the exposure and paying for losses out of pocket, which is the opposite of stopping the activity. Risk transfer through insurance shifts the financial burden to an insurer but the exposure would still exist if delivery continued. Contractual risk transfer shifts liability to another party by agreement, which does not apply since the activity was simply discontinued.
  209. The correct answer is D (Loss reduction): installing sprinklers and fire-resistant storage after a fire hazard is identified is a loss reduction measure, since it limits the severity of a fire that still might occur rather than preventing the exposure from existing at all. Risk avoidance would mean eliminating the warehouse operation entirely, which did not happen here. Non-insurance transfer shifts responsibility to another party by contract, not physical improvements. Full retention with no funding means accepting losses with no controls or reserves, which contradicts installing protective equipment.
  210. Contractual indemnification shifts the financial burden to another party by agreement, not an internal reserve. Risk avoidance would mean the trucking firm stops hauling cargo altogether, which is not described. Third-party additional insured status extends coverage to another party under someone else's policy, unrelated to an internal reserve.
  211. Unfunded retention, as described in the section covering definitions within the policy wording describes accepting losses with no reserve set aside, which contradicts shifting the exposure to the subcontractor.
  212. Aggregate stop-loss purchase is an insurance financing arrangement, unrelated to physical security upgrades. Contractual hold-harmless shifts liability to another party by agreement, which does not describe lighting or cameras.
  213. The correct answer is D (Insurance is only one financing tool within a broader program of identification and control): a complete risk management program includes exposure identification and physical or contractual controls in addition to insurance, so insurance alone is never the whole program. Insurance does not eliminate the need for physical loss prevention measures, since controls still reduce frequency and severity regardless of coverage. Insurance does not replace the need to review contracts for liability transfer, since contract wording still creates or shifts exposures. Issuing binding authority describes a delegated underwriting arrangement, not general risk advice to the client.

A subrogation agreement addresses an insurer's recovery rights against a third party, unrelated to continuity planning.
  217. It does not satisfy a co-insurance clause, which is a property policy requirement about insuring to an adequate percentage of value. It does not avoid the need for business interruption coverage, since some downtime and lost income can still occur even with alternates in place. It does not transfer liability to the alternate supplier, since the plan addresses continuity, not legal responsibility for a loss.

Verifying the named insured on a certificate is an administrative confirmation, not an evaluation of interruption severity. Filing a notice of claim happens after a loss occurs, not during exposure evaluation.

Loss prevention refers to physical measures that reduce the chance of a loss, not a decision about how to pay for it.

Frequency reduction targets how often a loss happens in the first place, which fire doors do not address. Complete exposure avoidance, provided the underwriter has confirmed this in writing beforehand would mean eliminating the fire hazard's source entirely, not containing it after ignition. Financial risk transfer shifts the cost of a loss to another party, unrelated to a physical containment measure.
  221.   223. The correct answer is C (Pure risk): a risk involving only the possibility of loss, with no chance of gain, is a pure risk, and it is the category that property and liability insurance is designed to address. Speculative risk includes the chance of gain as well as loss, such as a business investment, which is generally uninsurable. Investment risk is a form of speculative risk tied to financial markets, not the type insurance covers. Entrepreneurial risk similarly involves potential profit alongside potential loss, placing it outside standard insurable risk.
  224. The correct answer is A (Speculative risk): launching a new product line carries both the chance of profit and the chance of loss, which is the defining feature of speculative risk, and standard commercial policies are not designed to insure that kind of outcome. Pure risk involves only the possibility of loss with no chance of gain, which does not describe a business venture with upside potential. Fundamental risk affects large groups or society broadly rather than a single business decision. Particular risk affects one specific insured in a narrow way, not the general uninsurability of profit-seeking ventures.
  225. The correct answer is D (Fundamental risk): a widespread event like a regional flood that affects an entire community rather than one insured is classified as a fundamental risk because of its broad scope and impersonal cause. Particular risk affects a single insured or a narrow group, the opposite of a community-wide event. Speculative risk involves the chance of gain as well as loss, unrelated to a natural catastrophe. Contractual risk arises from obligations assumed in an agreement, not from a natural event affecting a whole region.
  226. The correct answer is D (A particular risk affecting one insured): a single vehicle striking a parked car is limited to one insured and one specific incident, which is the definition of a particular risk. A fundamental risk affects a broad group or the community at large, not one isolated collision. A speculative risk includes the chance of gain, which does not apply to an accidental collision. Describing it as a pure risk shared across an entire industry misses the point that this exposure is narrow and specific to one insured, not industry-wide.
  227. The correct answer is A (Matching the risk financing method to the frequency and severity of each exposure): retaining a high-frequency, low-severity exposure while transferring a low-frequency, high-severity one reflects the core principle of matching financing methods to each exposure's own frequency and severity profile. Avoiding every exposure regardless of cost ignores the fact that some exposures are worth keeping for the business's operations. Insuring every exposure at first-dollar coverage would be inefficient and expensive for predictable, low-cost losses. Transferring all exposures through a single blanket policy, based on the standard practice most brokers follow in this province ignores the different characteristics that make some exposures better suited to retention.

Avoidance does not always increase total insurance premium, since removing an exposure can actually reduce the premium needed for that operation. Avoidance is a valid and commonly used technique in hospitality when an exposure's cost outweighs its benefit, so it is not always inappropriate. Avoidance does not transfer the exposure to an insurer, since eliminating the activity means there is no longer an exposure to insure at all.

Small businesses are not automatically exempt from liability exposures simply because they employ fewer people. Formal identification is a sound business practice for any organization, not a requirement limited to publicly traded companies. Exposure identification is most valuable before a claim occurs, since its purpose is to prevent or prepare for losses rather than react to them.

The plan does not automatically expire in a way that voids the client's insurance policy, since the two are separate documents. Insurers do not typically require a brand new continuity plan before every renewal quote, though they may ask whether one exists.

The correct answer is B (Identify all the loss exposures the client's operations could realistically face): Exposure identification is always the first step in the risk management process because a broker cannot analyze, control, or finance a risk that has not yet been recognized. Negotiating discounts happens later, after coverage needs are known, not before exposures are identified. Buying a broad CGL policy without first identifying exposures risks gaps in coverage and unnecessary premium spent on irrelevant protection. Calculating the cost of risk is a monitoring activity that depends on exposures already having been identified and managed.

An interview alone gathers useful detail but does not provide the standardized comparison a checklist offers. Financial statement review identifies exposures tied to assets and revenue but is not built around coverage-to-exposure comparison. A physical inspection reveals hazards on site but, like the interview, lacks the checklist's standardized side-by-side structure.

A physical inspection uncovers hazards through direct observation of the premises, not accounting records. An organizational chart review focuses on personnel and reporting relationships, not asset values. A hazard and operability study examines process safety in technical systems, not financial figures.
  244. The correct answer is C (A flowchart mapping the production process to expose bottleneck dependencies): A flowchart traces each stage of the production process and shows where a single point of failure, like one plant with no backup, could halt the entire operation and cause a business interruption loss. A policy checklist compares coverage to typical exposures but does not map process dependencies. An exit interview gathers information about why an employee is leaving, unrelated to production bottlenecks. A general ledger review shows financial transactions but does not visually expose where a process could break down.

The correct answer is B (Conducting an on-site physical inspection of the warehouse premises): Only direct, physical observation of the premises can confirm housekeeping problems, blocked exits, and improper storage, since these hazards would not necessarily appear in written records or financial statements. Reviewing a certificate of insurance only shows what coverage exists, not the physical condition of the building. Analyzing the balance sheet reveals inventory values, not physical hazard conditions. A standard checklist flags typical exposures for the industry but cannot confirm the specific physical hazards actually present at this site.
  246. A debt-to-equity ratio analysis measures financial leverage, not the likelihood or size of future losses. An organizational chart review identifies personnel exposures but does not rank them by frequency and severity. A contract review uncovers contractual liability exposures but is not itself a ranking or prioritization tool.
  247. The correct answer is A (Avoidance, by choosing not to build at that flood-prone location): Avoidance eliminates a loss exposure entirely by not undertaking the activity that creates it, which is the only technique that removes the flood exposure completely rather than reducing or shifting it. Loss reduction with sump pumps only lessens the damage after a flood occurs, it does not prevent the exposure. Segregation spreads exposure units across locations but does not address the specific risk of building on a flood plain. Contractual transfer shifts financial responsibility to another party but the physical flood exposure still exists.

The correct answer is C (Loss reduction aimed at limiting the severity of a loss in progress): Sprinkler systems activate after a fire has already started and work to limit the size of the resulting damage, which is the defining feature of loss reduction rather than prevention. Avoidance would mean the company stops the warehouse activity altogether, which is not what is described here. Loss prevention focuses on stopping the fire from starting in the first place, such as removing ignition sources, not controlling it after ignition. Risk retention is a financing technique for funding losses, unrelated to physical loss control measures like sprinklers.
  249. The correct answer is C (Segregation of exposure units by spreading assets across separate locations): Segregation reduces the maximum possible loss from a single event by dividing exposure units, such as vehicles, across multiple separate locations, so one incident cannot destroy the entire fleet. Avoidance would mean the trucking activity itself is eliminated, which has not happened here. Loss prevention would involve reducing the chance of a fire starting, not spreading assets geographically. Contractual transfer shifts ownership or financial responsibility to another party, but the fleet remains owned and operated by the trucking company.
  250. The correct answer is B (Contractual transfer of liability exposure through a hold harmless agreement): A hold harmless or indemnification clause shifts the financial responsibility for certain liability exposures from one contracting party to another without changing who is actually at fault, which is the core feature of contractual risk transfer. Safety training is a loss prevention measure aimed at reducing the chance of an incident, not shifting financial responsibility by contract. Segregation involves spreading physical exposure units across locations, not shifting liability through an agreement. Risk retention means the contractor keeps the financial burden itself, which is the opposite of what this clause accomplishes.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Risk retention, funding predictable losses internally through a reserve account): Retention means the organization keeps the financial burden of a loss exposure and funds it internally, which matches the retailer setting aside its own reserve for predictable smaller losses. Avoidance would require the retailer to eliminate the property exposure entirely, which is not happening since stores continue operating. Contractual transfer would require another party, such as a landlord, to formally assume the financial risk, which is not described here. Loss prevention is a physical risk control measure, not a way of financing losses that do occur.
  2. The correct answer is D (Risk transfer through the purchase of commercial property insurance): Paying a premium so an insurer agrees to indemnify the manufacturer for a covered loss is the classic definition of risk transfer through commercial insurance, shifting the financial consequence of the loss to a third party. Retention would mean the manufacturer keeps and funds the exposure itself, which is the opposite of buying an indemnity contract. Segregation requires spreading exposure units across multiple locations, but the scenario describes only one facility. Avoidance would require the manufacturer to stop the production activity entirely, which has not occurred.
  3. The correct answer is D (A group captive insurance company owned by its member policyholders): A captive is an insurance company formed and owned by its insureds specifically to insure the risks of its owners, which matches unrelated companies pooling resources into a jointly owned insurer. A standard commercial policy is purchased from an unrelated insurer in the open market, not owned by the insureds themselves. A hold harmless agreement is a contractual transfer technique between two parties, not a formal insurance entity. Calling it something that avoids insurance entirely is incorrect, since a captive is itself a licensed insurance mechanism, just one owned by its policyholders.
  4. The correct answer is C (Insurance to value, carrying limits equal to the coinsurance percentage required): Coinsurance clauses require the insured to carry a limit equal to a stated percentage of the property's value, and failing to insure to that value results in a penalty applied proportionately to any claim payment. A deductible amount is a separate policy feature unrelated to the coinsurance percentage requirement. A named perils endorsement defines which causes of loss are covered, not the amount of insurance carried. A business interruption extension covers lost income, not the property valuation requirement that triggers the coinsurance penalty here.
  5. The correct answer is B (Construction, since the building's structural materials determine fire resistance): The Construction element of COPE examines the building materials and structural type, such as fire-resistive concrete versus combustible wood framing, because these materials directly affect how a fire will spread and how much damage will result. Occupancy refers to how the building is used and by whom, not what it is built from. Protection refers to fire suppression and detection systems and public fire department response, not the building's own structural materials. Exposure refers to external hazards from neighbouring properties, not the subject building's own construction.
  6. The correct answer is A (Occupancy, since the business activity conducted in the building creates the hazard): Occupancy in the COPE framework describes what activity takes place inside the building, and a bakery's open flames and grease buildup are hazards created directly by that business use. Construction refers to the physical building materials, not the activities the tenant conducts inside it. Protection refers to fire detection and suppression systems, not the nature of the tenant's operations. Exposure refers to external hazards from surrounding properties, not the hazard created by the occupant's own bakery activity.
  7. The correct answer is B (Protection, since these features reduce loss severity through detection and suppression): The Protection element of COPE covers both private fire protection, like sprinklers and monitored alarms, and public protection, like the proximity and quality of the responding fire department, all of which reduce the severity of a fire loss once it starts. Construction refers to the building's own structural materials, not detection or suppression systems. Occupancy refers to the business activity conducted inside the building, not the protective systems installed. Exposure describes external hazards from neighbouring properties, not the subject building's own protection features.
  8. The correct answer is D (Exposure, since a hazard from a neighbouring property threatens the subject building): The Exposure element of COPE refers specifically to external hazards from surrounding properties that could spread to or damage the subject building, exactly as a neighbouring fireworks facility could. Construction refers to the subject building's own structural materials, not a neighbour's building. Occupancy describes the activity of the business inside the subject building, in this case the retail store, not an external neighbour. Protection describes fire detection and suppression at the subject site, not conditions on the adjoining property.
  9. The correct answer is C (An indirect or consequential loss, resulting from the direct property damage): Lost revenue and extra expenses that follow from physical damage, without being the physical damage itself, are indirect or consequential losses, commonly insured through business interruption or extra expense coverage. The direct property loss is the physical damage to the building and machinery, separate from the resulting income loss described here. A liability loss would require a third party to bring a claim against the client, which is not part of this scenario. A personnel loss involves the loss of a key employee's services, not lost revenue from a property loss.
  10. The correct answer is A (A liability loss exposure arising from the store's premises operations): A third party's claim for bodily injury allegedly caused by the store's premises is a classic liability loss exposure, which is exactly what commercial general liability insurance is designed to address. A direct property loss involves physical damage to the store owner's own building or contents, not a third party's injury claim. A net income loss involves lost revenue from an interruption to operations, not a liability claim. Comparing conduct to a competitor's actions is not the recognized legal standard, since competitors could all fall short of the reasonable standard.   75. The correct answer is A (Causation, linking the defendant's breach to the claimant's actual harm): The but-for test asks whether the harm would have occurred but for the defendant's negligent conduct, which is precisely how Canadian courts establish causation. A contractual relationship is not required for a tort claim and is a separate legal concept from causation. The standard of care addresses what conduct was required, not whether that conduct actually produced the harm suffered. Vicarious liability concerns who bears responsibility for an employee's actions, not whether the conduct caused the injury.

The correct answer is C (Special damages, which represent quantifiable pre-trial financial losses): Special damages cover precisely calculable financial losses incurred before trial, such as lost wages and medical bills, distinguishing them from harder-to-quantify general damages like pain and suffering. Nominal damages are awarded when a technical wrong occurred without substantial measurable harm, which does not describe lost wages. Punitive damages punish egregious conduct rather than compensate for financial loss. Aggravated damages address exceptional distress from intentional or high-handed conduct, not routine calculable expenses.
  77. The correct answer is B (Vicarious liability, since the act occurred within the scope of employment): Vicarious liability holds an employer responsible for an employee's negligent acts committed within the scope of their employment duties, such as driving a scheduled delivery route. Contributory negligence apportions fault between the plaintiff and defendant, it does not create employer liability for an employee's conduct. Strict liability applies to specific activities regardless of fault and does not automatically apply to ordinary vehicle use. Res ipsa loquitur is an evidentiary inference used when the cause of harm is otherwise unexplained, not a standalone doctrine of employer responsibility.
  78. The correct answer is D (Liability is apportioned between both parties under contributory negligence rules): When both parties contributed to causing the harm, Canadian courts apportion liability under provincial contributory negligence legislation based on each party's relative fault. Assigning the pedestrian full liability ignores the driver's own speeding, which also contributed to the collision. Assigning the driver full liability ignores the pedestrian's own careless conduct in jaywalking mid-block. Contributory negligence does not eliminate liability entirely for either party, it apportions it rather than cancelling it out.
  79. The correct answer is A (Defamation, since a false published statement damaged the electrician's reputation): Defamation occurs when a false statement is published to a third party and causes harm to another person's reputation, exactly what happened with the false online review. Trespass concerns unlawful interference with land or property, not reputational harm from a statement. Conversion involves wrongfully taking or dealing with someone's personal property, not a false accusation. Nuisance concerns unreasonable interference with the use and enjoyment of land, not damage to a person's reputation.

The correct answer is D (A provincial superior court, which hears higher-value civil trial matters): Provincial superior courts have broad jurisdiction over significant civil disputes, including most commercial insurance litigation, and are the typical starting point for such trials. The Supreme Court of Canada only hears appeals granted leave, it is not a court of first instance for civil trials. A municipal bylaw tribunal handles local regulatory matters, not substantial commercial litigation. The Federal Court of Appeal reviews decisions from federal tribunals and the Federal Court, it does not try commercial disputes first.
  81. The doctrine of estoppel prevents a party from going back on a prior representation, it has nothing to do with foreseeability of harm. Privity restricts contractual claims to the parties to that contract, which is unrelated to tort liability for injury. The parol evidence rule concerns interpreting written contracts, not assessing the scope of tort liability.
  82. Absolute privilege applies to specific protected settings like parliamentary or judicial proceedings, not ordinary newspaper commentary. Consent requires the claimant to have actually agreed to the statement being made, which a contractor being criticized has not done. Volenti non fit injuria concerns voluntary assumption of physical risk, not exposure to published criticism.

A small claims adjudicator only handles lower-value original disputes, not appeals of legal errors. An administrative tribunal reviews regulatory decisions made by government bodies, not judicial trial rulings. The original trial court does not have jurisdiction to review its own decision for legal error, that role belongs to the appellate court.

Punishing the subcontractor describes punitive damages, which serve a different purpose than compensation. Quebec maintains a full court system and does not rely solely on federal arbitration for civil disputes. The duty to warn applies specifically to manufacturers regarding known product dangers. A fiduciary duty arises in relationships of trust and confidence, such as between a broker and client, not in assessing an injured claimant's own conduct.

Punitive damages punish particularly reckless or malicious conduct, not a case of minimal harm.   95. The correct answer is A (Tort law seeks to compensate the injured party, while criminal law punishes the offender): Tort law is a civil remedy aimed at compensating the injured party for their loss, while criminal law is a public prosecution aimed at punishing conduct that harms society. Tort claims are decided on a balance of probabilities, a lower standard than the criminal standard of proof beyond a reasonable doubt. Tort actions are brought by the private injured party, not prosecuted by the Crown on behalf of the public. Imprisonment is a criminal law consequence, while successful tort claims result in a damages award rather than jail time.

Duress concerns being forced to act against one's will, which does not apply to voluntarily made factual statements. Absolute certainty is not a standard used anywhere in Canadian law since it is practically unattainable. Clear and convincing evidence exclusively is not the general civil standard applied in Canadian tort claims.
  115. Subrogation against a third party concerns an insurer recovering its payout, unrelated to apportioning fault between the pedestrian and driver.   127.   128. Indemnification alone describes the insurer's payment obligation to the insured, not its right to recover from a third party. Contribution among insurers applies when multiple insurers cover the same risk, not recovery from a negligent outside party. Assignment of benefits involves a policyholder transferring claim rights to someone else, which is a different mechanism entirely.
  129. The correct answer is A (Indemnity): this core insurance principle ensures a claim payment restores the insured to their pre-loss financial position without producing a profit from the loss. Utmost good faith between the parties concerns honest disclosure obligations, not the measure of claim payment. Subrogation against a wrongdoer concerns the insurer's recovery rights after paying a claim, not the principle limiting the payment itself.
