  136. The correct answer is D (Res ipsa loquitur): this doctrine, meaning the thing speaks for itself, permits an inference of negligence where the accident is of a type that ordinarily would not occur without carelessness, even absent direct proof of who was at fault. Strict liability for hazardous storage would require the activity itself to be inherently hazardous by classification, which is not established here. Vicarious liability of the warehouse owner concerns responsibility for a specific identified employee's conduct, which is exactly what cannot be pinpointed in this scenario. Contributory negligence of the passerby is not indicated, since nothing suggests the passerby acted carelessly.
  137. The correct answer is C (Special damages): these are precisely quantifiable financial losses, such as medical bills and lost wages, that can be calculated and documented with specific evidence like receipts and pay records. General damages for pain and suffering are estimated rather than precisely calculated, unlike the documented losses described here. Punitive damages meant to punish are awarded to penalize egregious conduct, not to compensate for measurable financial loss. Aggravated damages for humiliation address the manner in which harm was inflicted, not straightforward financial losses.
  138. The correct answer is A (General damages): these compensate for non-monetary losses like pain, suffering, and diminished quality of life that cannot be precisely calculated from documentation. Special damages proven by receipts refer to quantifiable financial losses like medical bills, the opposite of what is described here. Nominal damages of a token amount are awarded when a legal right was violated but no substantial loss occurred, which does not fit a genuine injury claim. Liquidated damages fixed by contract are a pre-agreed contractual remedy, unrelated to a tort injury award.
  139. The correct answer is D (Punitive damages): these are awarded separately from compensatory amounts specifically to punish and deter particularly reprehensible conduct, such as bad faith claims handling. Special damages for financial loss compensate quantifiable losses, not punish wrongful conduct. Nominal damages of a token sum are awarded for a technical legal violation without real loss, unrelated to punishing bad faith. General damages for suffering compensate the claimant's own hardship rather than penalize the wrongdoer's behaviour.

Option B, subrogation, describes the insurer's right to pursue a third party responsible for the loss after paying a claim, which is a different procedure entirely. The correct answer is A (A bill of lading, serving as receipt, contract evidence, and document of title): a bill of lading performs three functions at once, acting as a receipt for goods shipped, evidence of the terms of the contract of carriage, and a negotiable document of title that can transfer ownership of the goods. Option B, an open cover, is an insurance arrangement providing automatic coverage across future shipments and has none of the bill of lading's documentary functions.   250. Option A, the doctrine of general average, addresses proportional contribution following a voluntary sacrifice and has no connection to a vessel's fitness for the voyage. Option C, subrogation, describes an insurer's right to pursue a responsible third party after paying a claim, unrelated to seaworthiness. Option D, the sue and labour obligation, requires the insured to mitigate an existing loss, which is a distinct concept from the vessel's fitness at the start of the voyage.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Deviation without lawful excuse can discharge the insurer from liability from that point): an unauthorized deviation from the agreed or customary route, without lawful excuse, can discharge the insurer from liability for loss occurring after the point of deviation under marine insurance principles. Option A is incorrect because marine policies are in fact written with reference to a specific insured voyage or route, and departing from it carries real consequences. Option C incorrectly claims coverage would double, when in fact an unauthorized deviation can reduce or eliminate coverage rather than expand it. Option D is incorrect because deviation principles apply to both hull and cargo policies, not to hull policies alone.
  2. The correct answer is A (The York-Antwerp Rules, the widely adopted framework for general average adjustment): the York-Antwerp Rules are the internationally recognized set of rules that average adjusters apply to calculate and apportion general average sacrifices and expenditures among the interests in a maritime venture. Option B, the Institute Cargo Clauses, define the scope of insured cargo perils rather than provide a framework for adjusting general average. Option C, the Marine Insurance Act, governs the formation and interpretation of marine insurance contracts generally, not the specific mechanics of general average apportionment. Option D, the Hague-Visby Rules, primarily govern a carrier's liability under a bill of lading rather than the calculation of general average.
  3. The correct answer is D (A salvage award, compensation for voluntarily and successfully saving property in peril): under maritime law, a party that voluntarily and successfully renders assistance to a vessel in distress, without a pre-existing duty to do so, is entitled to a salvage award reflecting the value of the service rendered. Option A, general average contribution, is a proportional payment owed among interests following a voluntary sacrifice made for the common venture, not a reward for an outside rescuer. Option B, freight compensation, addresses a shipowner's lost freight earnings and has no bearing on a rescuing vessel's entitlement. Option C, sue and labour reimbursement, covers reasonable mitigation expenses incurred by an insured after a loss, not a reward for a third party's rescue.
  4. The correct answer is C (An open cargo cover, providing automatic coverage on a continuous series of shipments): an open cargo cover is a standing arrangement that automatically extends coverage to each qualifying shipment as it occurs, eliminating the need to arrange a separate policy for every individual movement of goods. Option A, a time policy, insures a vessel's hull for a fixed period and has no application to insuring an importer's ongoing cargo shipments. Option B, a single voyage policy, covers only one specific shipment and would not meet the client's need for automatic, ongoing coverage. Option D, a charter party agreement, governs the hire of a vessel and is unrelated to arranging cargo insurance coverage.
  5. The correct answer is D (Inland marine insurance, covering transit and certain property risks not on the ocean): inland marine insurance covers property in transit by land, along with certain mobile equipment and specified property classes, extending marine insurance concepts to risks that never involve an ocean voyage. Option A, hull insurance, is specifically designed to cover an ocean going vessel's own structure and has no application to truck and rail transit. Option B, ocean marine insurance, applies to vessels and cargo on international sea voyages, which does not match the entirely land based transit described. Option C, protection and indemnity cover, addresses a shipowner's third party liabilities and has no relevance to land based transit or mobile equipment.
  6. The correct answer is C (A marine surveyor, an independent professional who inspects and reports on cargo loss): a marine surveyor is an independent, qualified professional engaged to inspect damaged cargo, determine the cause and extent of the loss, and produce a report that supports the claim assessment process. Option A, the average adjuster, is primarily responsible for apportioning general average losses rather than conducting the physical inspection of damaged cargo. Option B, the ship's master, has an inherent conflict of interest in assessing a loss that may relate to the vessel's own handling of the cargo. Option D, the reinsurer, is typically several steps removed from the individual claim and is not the party who conducts a physical cargo inspection.
  7. The correct answer is B (Subrogation, which allows the insurer to pursue recovery from the responsible third party): subrogation allows an insurer, after paying a claim, to step into the insured's legal position and pursue recovery from a third party, such as a negligent carrier, that caused the loss. Option A, abandonment, allows the insured to surrender remaining rights in damaged property to the insurer following a constructive total loss, a different procedure entirely. Option C, barratry, describes wrongful conduct by a vessel's master or crew and has no connection to an insurer's recovery rights. Option D, general average, requires proportional contribution among interests following a voluntary sacrifice, not a claim against a negligent carrier.
  8. The correct answer is A (Implied warranties, which attach automatically by operation of marine insurance law): implied warranties, such as seaworthiness and legality of the adventure, attach to a marine policy automatically by operation of law regardless of whether they are expressly written into the policy wording. Option B, endorsed warranties, would need to be specifically added by the broker or insurer, which contradicts the automatic nature of the warranties described. Option C, express warranties, must always be explicitly stated in the policy, the opposite of the implied warranties being described here. Option D, void warranties, is a fabricated category with no legal meaning under the Marine Insurance Act.
  9. The correct answer is A (A partial loss affects only part of the value, unlike a complete total loss): a partial loss affects only a portion of the value of the insured subject matter, in contrast to a total loss, whether actual or constructive, which involves the complete destruction or irretrievable loss of the whole. Option B is incorrect because a partial loss and a total loss are settled differently and are not treated identically for claim purposes. Option C incorrectly restricts partial losses to cargo alone, when a vessel can also suffer a partial loss such as localized hull damage. Option D is incorrect because partial losses are routinely covered, not excluded, under standard marine insurance policies.
  10. The correct answer is B (Institute Cargo Clauses (B), a moderate named peril cover between A and C): ICC(B) provides a moderate list of named perils that is broader than the narrow Clause C list but narrower than the all risks scope of Clause A, making it the appropriate middle option for this client. Option A, Institute Cargo Clauses (A), provides the broadest all risks cover, which exceeds what the client says they need. Option C, Institute Time Clauses (Hulls), is a vessel hull form and has no application to insuring cargo. Option D, Institute Cargo Clauses (C), provides only the narrowest named peril list, which the client has already said is insufficient.
  11. The correct answer is C (Utmost good faith, requiring full and honest disclosure of all material facts): marine insurance contracts have historically been governed by the principle of utmost good faith, requiring both parties, and particularly the insured, to disclose all material facts honestly and completely before the contract is concluded. Option A, subrogation, concerns an insurer's right to recover from a responsible third party after paying a claim, a separate concept from pre-contract disclosure. Option B, indemnity, limits recovery to the actual financial loss suffered and does not address disclosure obligations. Option D, contribution, apportions a loss among multiple insurers covering the same risk and is unrelated to the duty of disclosure.
  12. The correct answer is D (A time policy, providing coverage for a fixed period regardless of voyages made): a time policy provides coverage over a defined period of time, regardless of how many voyages the vessel completes during that period, which matches the shipowner's stated need. Option A, a charter party, is a contract for hiring the vessel itself, not a type of insurance policy. Option B, a voyage policy, ties coverage to a single specific trip between two ports rather than a fixed time period. Option C, an open cargo cover, provides automatic coverage for a series of cargo shipments, not coverage for the vessel's hull over a time period.
  13. The correct answer is D (A valued policy, where the parties agree the insured value in advance): a valued policy fixes an agreed value for the insured subject matter at the outset of the contract, and that agreed figure is used to settle a total loss claim without the need for a fresh valuation afterward. Option A, an unvalued policy, leaves the value to be determined only after a loss occurs, the opposite of what is described here. Option B, a time policy, addresses the duration of coverage rather than how the insured value is fixed. Option C, an open cover, is a standing arrangement for automatic coverage across future shipments and does not itself address how value is agreed.
  14. The correct answer is C (A franchise clause, below which no claim is paid but above which the full loss pays): a franchise clause sets a threshold below which no claim is payable at all, but once the loss exceeds that threshold, the full amount of the loss is paid rather than only the excess above the threshold. Option A, a coinsurance clause, requires the insured to carry a stated percentage of full value and operates differently from a pay-or-nothing threshold. Option B, a deductible clause, always subtracts a fixed amount from every claim regardless of size, unlike a franchise which pays the full loss once the threshold is crossed. Option D, an excess clause, has the insured retain the first layer of every loss rather than paying the full amount once a threshold is exceeded.
  15. The correct answer is A (Adding the Institute War Clauses, since standard cargo cover excludes war risks): standard institute cargo clauses exclude loss or damage caused by war and related hostile acts, so a client shipping through an active conflict region needs the Institute War Clauses added to restore protection against those specific perils. Option B is incorrect because the sue and labour clause addresses mitigation expenses after a loss and does not reinstate an excluded peril category like war. Option C is incorrect because adjusting the deductible has no effect on which perils are covered in the first place. Option D is incorrect because switching to a time policy changes the duration basis of cover, not whether war risks are included.
  16. The correct answer is B (The strikes, riots and civil commotions clause, extending cover for those specific perils): the strikes, riots and civil commotions clause is the specific additional coverage that extends cargo protection to losses arising from those particular perils, which standard institute cargo clauses do not automatically include. Option A, the running down clause, addresses a shipowner's liability arising from a vessel collision and has nothing to do with strikes or civil unrest. Option C, the warehouse to warehouse clause, extends the geographic and time scope of cargo cover but does not add coverage for strikes or riots. Option D, the abandonment clause, allows surrender of remaining rights in damaged cargo following a constructive total loss, an unrelated concept.
  17. The correct answer is C (Named perils require the loss to match a listed cause, unlike broader all risks cover): under a named peril form, the insured loss must fall within one of the specifically listed causes to be covered, while an all risks form covers any fortuitous physical loss or damage except for stated exclusions, giving it broader practical scope. Option A is incorrect because the two approaches produce meaningfully different practical protection, which is the whole point of comparing them. Option B is incorrect because pricing depends on the specific risk and terms, not a fixed rule that all risks is always cheaper. Option D is incorrect because an all risks form does not exclude perils a named peril form would include, it instead covers a broader range subject only to its own stated exclusions.
  18. The correct answer is B (Institute Yacht Clauses, designed specifically for pleasure craft rather than trading vessels): the Institute Yacht Clauses are the specialized marine clause set designed for the particular risks and usage patterns of personal pleasure craft, distinct from clauses written for commercial trading vessels. Option A, Institute Cargo Clauses, insures goods carried aboard a vessel and has no application to insuring the pleasure craft itself. Option C, Institute War Clauses, address hostilities exclusions across various marine classes and are not a standalone hull form for a yacht. Option D, Institute Time Clauses (Hulls), is designed primarily for commercial trading vessels and does not reflect the specialized terms a personal pleasure craft requires.
  19. The correct answer is D (A general average contribution, the proportional share owed by each saved interest): a general average contribution is the proportional payment that each interest whose property was saved must make toward the sacrifice and expenditure, calculated by the average adjuster under a recognized framework such as the York-Antwerp Rules. Option A, a salvage award, compensates a party that voluntarily rescues a vessel or cargo, a different concept from a proportional contribution among saved interests. Option B, a franchise deduction, applies only when a loss falls below a stated policy threshold and is unrelated to general average apportionment. Option C, a subrogation recovery, is pursued by an insurer against a negligent third party rather than owed by the saved cargo interests themselves.

Option C incorrectly claims only arbitration under the Marine Insurance Act is available, when the Federal Court has recognized jurisdiction over such matters. Protection and indemnity insurance covers a shipowner's third party liabilities, not a shipper's own goods in a purely domestic truck movement.
  22. The correct answer is D (Institute Cargo Clauses (A), which cover all risks of physical loss or damage, subject to standard exclusions): Clause A provides the broadest available standard cargo coverage, insuring against all risks of physical loss or damage subject to a limited list of standard exclusions, making it the appropriate choice for fragile, high value goods. Clause C covers only a short list of major named casualties and Clause B covers a wider but still limited named peril list, both narrower than Clause A. Institute War Clauses address only war related perils and would not stand alone as the primary cargo coverage.
  23. The correct answer is A (As a general average loss, shared proportionally among the ship, cargo, and freight interests): a voluntary sacrifice made intentionally to preserve the ship and cargo from a common peril is the defining feature of general average, and the resulting loss is shared proportionally by all parties who benefited from the sacrifice. A particular average loss falls only on one specific interest rather than being shared, which does not match a deliberate common sacrifice. It is not a total loss since only part of the cargo was sacrificed, and jettison for the common safety is a recognized and insurable general average act, not an automatic exclusion.
  24. The correct answer is C (A particular average loss, a partial loss borne solely by the specific interest affected): because only one container was damaged while all other cargo interests were unaffected, this loss falls entirely on that one owner rather than being shared, which is the hallmark of a particular average loss. General average requires a deliberate sacrifice made for the common safety of the whole venture, which is not what happened here since the seawater entry was accidental. Salvage and sue and labour both relate to different concepts entirely, namely a voluntary rescue reward and protective expenses respectively.
  25. The correct answer is B (The sue and labour clause, covering reasonable costs incurred to minimize a covered loss): this clause specifically allows the insured, or their agent, to recover reasonable expenses incurred in an effort to avert or minimize a loss that is otherwise covered by the policy. The general average clause applies to sacrifices made for the whole voyage, not unilateral post loss mitigation by one cargo interest. The abandonment clause and warehouse to warehouse clause address entirely different matters, namely transferring a total loss claim and extending the geographic period of coverage.
  26. The correct answer is A (A constructive total loss, where the cost of repair or recovery exceeds the insured value): when the cost to repair or recover a damaged vessel would exceed its insured value once restored, the owner may treat the loss as a constructive total loss and abandon the vessel to the insurer. An actual total loss requires the vessel to be physically destroyed or irretrievably lost, which has not happened here since repair remains physically possible. A particular average loss and a general average loss both describe partial or shared losses, not a situation where the vessel is being treated as a total loss.
  27. The correct answer is D (Give proper and timely notice of abandonment to the insurer within a reasonable time): to claim as though a total loss occurred following a constructive total loss, the insured must give the insurer proper and timely notice of abandonment, transferring the remaining rights in the subject matter to the insurer. Filing a proof of loss with a shipping registry has no bearing on this insurance right, and waiting passively for the insurer to act does not preserve the insured's claim. Selling the wreck before giving notice would improperly dispose of property the insurer may be entitled to under the abandonment.
  28. The correct answer is C (The implied warranty of seaworthiness, which requires the vessel to be fit for that voyage): a vessel lacking sufficient crew, equipment, and provisions for the specific voyage undertaken is unseaworthy, breaching the implied warranty that the vessel be reasonably fit in all respects for that voyage. The warranty of legality concerns whether the adventure itself is lawful, and the warranty against deviation concerns route rather than the vessel's condition, neither of which describes this defect. There is no general warranty of good faith requiring disclosure of claims history under marine insurance principles in this context.
  29. The correct answer is A (Deviation, which can discharge the insurer from liability for loss occurring from that point onward): an unauthorized departure from the agreed or customary route, without a lawful excuse, is deviation, and it can discharge the insurer's liability for loss occurring after the deviation begins. Barratry describes wrongful conduct by the master or crew against the owner's interests, which is a different concept from an unauthorized route change. Jettison and salvage describe casting cargo overboard and a reward for a voluntary rescue, neither of which describes an unauthorized change of course.
  30. The correct answer is D (Barratry, a wilful wrongful act committed by the master or crew to the shipowner's detriment): secretly selling cargo for personal gain and deliberately scuttling the vessel are wilful wrongful acts committed by the master against the shipowner's own interests, which is the classic definition of barratry. General average involves a voluntary sacrifice made for the common safety, the opposite of a self interested wrongful act. Perils of the sea and inherent vice both describe fortuitous or natural causes of loss, not deliberate wrongdoing by the crew.
  31. The correct answer is B (Perils of the sea, meaning fortuitous accidents or casualties of the seas beyond ordinary wear): an unusually violent storm causing structural hull failure and water ingress is a fortuitous accident of the sea, the traditional definition of perils of the sea. Inherent vice refers to a defect in the goods themselves rather than an external event, and ordinary wear and tear describes gradual deterioration rather than a sudden storm casualty. Delay refers to loss of market value from late arrival, which is unrelated to physical storm damage.

The general average clause deals with contribution to a shared sacrifice, an unrelated concept.

A voyage policy instead covers only one specific defined trip, the opposite of what is described. An unvalued policy instead leaves the value to be determined only after a loss occurs, the opposite arrangement. The warehouse to warehouse clause extends cargo coverage during inland transit, an unrelated hull concept.
  36. The seller no longer bears the risk of physical loss after that point and therefore no longer holds the relevant insurable interest for the ocean voyage. The carrier does not automatically bear all risk of the cargo simply by carrying it, and insurable interest clearly exists in this scenario rather than existing for no one.

Paying the full loss regardless of underinsurance would ignore the whole purpose of the average clause, and denying the claim entirely is not how underinsurance is treated. The insurer does not simply pay the gap between insured amount and full value, since that describes a different, incorrect calculation.

  47. The correct answer is B (The York-Antwerp Rules, an internationally adopted framework governing general average adjustments worldwide): these internationally recognized rules were developed specifically to provide a uniform framework for adjusting general average claims across different countries involved in an international voyage. The Institute Cargo Clauses set out the scope of standard cargo coverage rather than the mechanics of general average adjustment, and the Hague Rules instead govern a carrier's liability under a bill of lading. The Marine Insurance Act is a domestic statute of one country rather than an internationally adopted adjustment framework.

  52. The principle of contribution and the principle of indemnity address allocating a loss among multiple policies and limiting recovery to actual loss respectively, neither of which describes a pre-contract disclosure failure.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (The war risk and allied perils buy-back endorsement, since hijacking is normally excluded from the base): hijacking and unlawful seizure fall within the broad war and allied perils exclusion in most base aviation policies and are only covered when the specific buy-back endorsement is purchased. Option A is incorrect because these perils are commonly excluded outright, not automatically included in the base grant. Option C addresses passenger injury claims, not the actual aircraft seizure itself. Option D compensates for medical unfitness, entirely unrelated to a hijacking event.
  2. The correct answer is A (The loss would almost certainly be excluded, since nuclear contamination is a standard exclusion): nuclear energy risks are universally excluded from aviation and most other property and liability policies because of their catastrophic and largely uninsurable nature at the primary market level. Option B misapplies a passenger injury coverage onto what is really a property exposure near the aircraft. Option C wrongly assumes hangarkeepers liability could somehow override a standard policy exclusion. Option D is incorrect because the in-flight classification does not itself remove an otherwise applicable exclusion.
  3.   32. The correct answer is D (The surety expects the principal to complete the obligation entirely without loss and will seek full reimbursement if it ever has to pay a claim.): The lead-in identifies the credit-based nature of surety underwriting. A surety views the principal much like a lender does, assessing character, capacity, and capital, because it fully expects a zero-loss outcome and will pursue recovery from the principal under the general indemnity agreement if it ever has to pay a claim. Option A is wrong because surety premiums are not priced from actuarial loss tables the way personal auto insurance is; surety underwriting is a credit decision, not an actuarial one. Option B misapplies auto coverage, unrelated to a cleanup order. Option C misapplies crime coverage, an unrelated exposure. Option D misapplies management liability, not designed for environmental remediation obligations.
  246. The correct answer is C (Coverage depends on the claim being made and reported during the current policy period): under a claims-made EIL policy, coverage responds when a claim is first made and reported during the policy period, provided the pollution condition falls on or after the retroactive date, regardless of exactly when the contamination technically began. Option A wrongly assumes automatic denial despite a satisfied retroactive date. Option B invents an irrelevant property sale condition. Option D addresses limit exhaustion, a separate issue from the triggering mechanism.
  247. The correct answer is A (Transportation pollution liability coverage, often added to auto or EIL policies): transportation pollution liability specifically addresses pollution conditions arising during the transport of goods, such as a chemical spill from a rollover, and is commonly added as an endorsement to either a commercial auto policy or a standalone environmental impairment liability policy. Option B misapplies property coverage, unrelated to a highway spill. Option C misapplies employment practices coverage, an unrelated exposure. Option D misapplies management liability, not designed for transportation pollution incidents.
  248. The correct answer is C (Some EIL policies offer a business interruption extension, but it is not automatic and should be confirmed): business interruption coverage tied to a covered pollution condition is often available as an optional extension on an EIL policy, but it is not a standard automatic feature, so the broker must confirm whether it has actually been purchased. Option A overstates coverage as universally automatic. Option B wrongly claims coverage never exists. Option D misapplies auto coverage, unrelated to this exposure.
  249. The correct answer is D (The lender's financial interest in the property against environmental contamination risk): lenders financing industrial or commercial property often require borrowers to carry pollution legal liability coverage, sometimes naming the lender as an additional insured, to protect the collateral's value against the financial impact of environmental contamination. Option A misapplies personal auto coverage, unrelated to this financing arrangement. Option B misapplies workplace injury coverage, an unrelated exposure. Option C misapplies management liability, unrelated to property collateral protection.
  250. The correct answer is B (Confirm both policies' definitions, exclusions, and limits align to avoid a coverage gap): given the absolute pollution exclusion typical in CGL policies, a broker must carefully cross-check the standalone EIL policy's own definitions, exclusions, and limits against the client's actual operations to ensure no coverage gap remains between the two policies. Option A wrongly assumes automatic coordination without verification. Option C would eliminate needed coverage rather than closing a gap. Option D would arbitrarily reduce coverage rather than aligning it appropriately.
